### Page 1

1

SPECIAL EDITION | HOW BAS IS THE RISE 
IN BOND YIELDS?

TRANSCRIPTION

0:00:10 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Everyone, welcome. As we head into autumn 2026, interest rates are reaching levels never seen for many, many years. 
Why is this happening? Is it sustainable? And what are the implications for the economies of different countries? All these 
questions will be discussed over the next few minutes with the economists of BNP Paribas. Let us begin with Isabelle 
Mateos y Lago, chief economist of the group. Hello, Isabelle.  
0:00:38 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everyone. 
0:00:39 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you for being with us today. Just to begin, a simple explanation. What rates are we talking about and which countries 
are affected? 
0:00:48 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, it is very important to clarify this. So, we are talking about long-term interest rates, rates at 10 years or longer 
maturities, 30 years, etc. The rates at which sovereign states borrow on the market. And this is primarily something that is 
happening in advanced countries, so United States, United King dom, Japan, and then the Eurozone countries. And indeed, 
as you said, we are seeing since the beginning of the summer, interest rates reach levels we had not seen for about 20 
years. But it is important to keep in mind that up until that point, up until the mid -2000s, the level of interest rates that 
we're seeing now were really quite common, quite normal, so one shouldn't overdramatize.  
0:01:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why do we find ourselves in this situation and how did we get to this point?  
0:01:41 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, so the direct cause is that investors are demanding higher interest rates in order to  lend their capital to governments. 
But why is this? Well, there's essentially three reasons for this, and not all of them are bad, and they're not mutually 
exclusive either. The top reason to my mind is that there's just greater competition for capital be tween sovereign states 
and the corporate sector, who are all facing simultaneously additional investment needs, in defense, in artificial 
intelligence, in energy transition, in resilience. And so,  you just have greater competition for capital and that pushes up the 
price of capital, which is the interest rate. 
Reason number two is because markets have revised upwards their expectations of how high central banks are going to be 
taking their interest rates. 
And this mechanically translates into higher rates at the long end. And then there's a third reason, which doesn't apply 
necessarily in every case, but which is essentially higher sovereign risk, a kind of a risk premium, in cases where there are  
concerns arising about the sustainability of these public debts.

### Page 2

2

0:02:59 - Emmanuel Laborde 
What are the implications for global economies? 
0:03:03 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
So that's exactly what we'll be discussing in the next segments. But basically, the two key issues are, one, an adverse 
impact on public finances. It's higher interest rates making it harder to balance the books in terms of public accounts. And 
then secondly, these higher rates tend to slow down economic growth. Of course, by how much and how serious it will be, 
depends on how high interest rates go and how long they stay there. But that's for the next segments.  
0:03:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Yes. Thank you very much, Isabelle. Having said the scene now, let's move on to the first segment with the following 
question. What makes debt sustainable? And let's see what Hélène Baudchon has to say.  
Hello Hélène and welcome into this special edition. 
0:03:58 - Hélène Baudchon 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everybody. 
0:04:01 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When economists talk about public debt, they often refer to the sustainability of debt. As an economist, can you explain to 
us what that means and what is the debt trajectory deemed unsustainable?  
0:04:15 - Hélène Baudchon 
To begin with, I will say that a temporary increase in debt can be seen as a normal evolution, not particularly concerning, 
for instance during an economic crisis or more recently during a health crisis. In such cases, it acts as a buffer. A problem  
arises when the debt level never comes back down. An unsustainable trajectory can be therefore defined, characterized by 
a continuous regular rise in the debt level, both in absolute terms and as a proportion of GDP, driven by high and recurring 
fiscal deficits. And sometimes interest payments can exacerbate the problem.  
0:05:00 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In very simple terms, you are talking about the snowball effect.  
0:05:03 - Hélène Baudchon 
Exactly explaining why consolidation measures are needed in order to prevent such an adverse situation from occurring 
and in order to prevent, in fact, a sudden loss of confidence from creditors.  
0:05:16 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Regarding governments, what should serve as a warning sign for them? And at what point could the situation become, let's 
say, problematic? 
0:05:27 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, simply speaking, when interest rates rise, not so long ago, up until 2022, Eurozone countries were borrowing at very 
low rates and in some instance, even negative rates. And although debt levels were already at high level at that time, the 
low rates helped manage debt servicing. Not much fiscal effort was needed to keep the public debt ratios in check.  
0:05:55 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Of course, we all remember this because it was a very unusual situation, but now things are very different.

### Page 3

3

0:06:01 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed, the situation today is clearly more challenging simply because bond yields are significantly higher and they 
are rising at the time of our discussion. This is for the part the result of the ECB's monetary tightening in 2022 -2023 aimed 
at addressing the inflation shock then. Then long-term interest rates have risen as a consequence and have aligned more 
closely with GDP growth rate. And in technical terms, this is what we refer to as a narrowing of the "r" - "g" spread. 
0:06:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why is this spread so important? Why do you, the economists, monitor it this way?  
0:06:48 - Hélène Baudchon 
Essentially, you have the interest rate "r", which determines the debt burden, while the growth rate "g" determines the 
trajectory of fiscal revenues. And if "r" is lower than "g", and this has been the case for many years, as you can see on the  
chart on the screen, it means that interest payments are increasing at a slower pace than the revenue component of the 
equation. 
0:07:14 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this provides some leeway in other budgetary areas, such as education, of course, defense, maybe pensions.  
0:07:22 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed. And this is what we refer to as the primary budget. However, if and when interest rates do go up while the 
economy isn't growing that much faster, then fiscal leeway diminishes.  
0:07:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Crystal clear. Now, just could you provide a few last words to wrap up the sustainability segment?  
0:07:43 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, to conclude, we are entering a period which demands greater fiscal discipline or, as some might say, less indiscipline 
in order to manage debt trajectories effectively, in particular in the countries which have high debt levels. And for instanc e, 
the Italian case is an interesting one because it is well known for its high debt level ratio. But in fact, it also has an 
advantageous position because it has no primary deficit. In fact, it even benefits from a large primary surplus.  
0:08:22 - Emmanuel Laborde 
As for the countries... For the other countries... I stop you because this will be a discussion with Guillaume Derrien during  
the next segment. Thank you very much, Hélène. 
0:08:30 - Hélène Baudchon 
Thank you, Emmanuel. 
0:08:36 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Guillaume. Welcome. 
0:08:37 - Guillaume Derrien 
Thank you. 
0:08:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Emmanuel, and hello to everyone. The flexibility of fiscal policy depends heavily on interest rates, which have 
increased considerably over the past few months. The question is, where do we stand today?

### Page 4

4

0:08:52 - Guillaume Derrien 
So, long-term rates have increased in all advanced economies, reaching levels not seen since 2008 in Europe and in the 
United States, and even since 1996 in Japan. Fortunately, the effective rate that states pay on their debt is lower than thos e 
headline figures suggest, simply because the average maturity of debt is relatively long. Precisely how long are we talking 
about? So, it's about 10 years in Japan, 13 years in the United Kingdom, and 8.5 years in France. That means that most of 
the debt was raised before rates started climbing. 
For example, in France, the effective rate just recently rose above the 2% mark, which is significantly lower than the 10 -
year bond yield, which exceeded 4% for new issuances.  
That said, in the United States, which has an average maturity of roughly six years, is experiencing a more rapid increase in  
its effective rate. 
0:09:54 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this rate increase is happening in the context of high debt -to-GDP ratios and with modest growth, correct? Yes, that's 
correct. 
0:10:05 - Guillaume Derrien 
Debt levels have reached unprecedented heights since the end of World War II. And at the same time, growth is lower, 
hovering around 2% on average in the United States, 1% in Europe and 0.5% in Japan. What does that mean for debt 
servicing costs? So, debt servicing cost is already relatively high, but it is expected to grow further as debt issue before the 
2022 rate hikes mature and are replaced with new instruments bearing higher interest rates. By 2030, interest costs could 
rise to levels last seen in 2000, specifically in France, around 4% of GDP. 
Are all the countries equally affected by these trends? Actually, no. Some countries are managing to reduce their debt 
level. 
Spain, Italy, even Japan have sufficiently improved their primary balance to achieve this, at least for the time being. And o f 
course, we must speak about Germany. Yes, Germany is implementing investment programs that result in a higher public 
deficit and debt-to-GDP ratio. But this ratio started from a relatively low base at around above 60% of GDP. That said, in 
this current context, German bonds have risen. And because German bonds are used as a benchmark for Europe, it  raises 
rates across all Eurozone countries. Is France the only country seeing its public debt go up?  
No, it's not, Emmanuel. This is happening in the United Kingdom and in the United States. These economies, like France, 
are running public deficits that are ever close or exceed 5% of GDP, which is too large for debt ratio to stabilize on its ow n. 
France and the United Kingdom are trying to gradually reduce their public deficit so as not to compromise growth. The 
United States is not going on that path yet, but market pressure could eventually force them to do so.  
0:12:08 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much for your input, Guillaume. Thank you, Emmanuel. Let's go to the next segment. It's about the impact 
of rising bond yields on financing and investments in the Eurozone.  
0:12:26 - Thomas Humblot 
Hello, Thomas. Welcome. Hello. Thank you for having me today.  
0:12:30 - Emmanuel Laborde 
We've just seen what's behind the rise in Eurozone bond yields. This rise is partly attributed to the sovereign spread. What 
impact will this have on the economy? 
0:12:42 - Thomas Humblot 
Actually, this widening of some government bond spreads is obviously not good news, but it tends paradoxically to have 
less impact than an increase in long-term interest rates that does not include the sovereign risk premium. for example, in 
France, for a very long time, it has been considered that the 10 -year OAT was the relevant benchmark for fixed loans. And 
at that time, the spread on French government bonds was relatively stable. But since 2024, that spread increased

### Page 5

5

significantly and that renders the sovereign yield a less relevant benchmark.  
And now the most relevant benchmark is the swap rate because it determines the cost of bank funding that backs new 
loans. 
0:13:56 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Since the end of 2024, we have seen a rise in long-term 10-year swap rates. What impact does this have on bank lending 
rates? 
0:14:08 - Thomas Humblot 
Fixed loans have obviously become more expensive, but to a lesser extent than if they were priced solely based on long -
term sovereign rates. And what about existing fixed-rate loans? 
There is no impact. An increase in rates for the entire maturity of a loan does not have any effect if it is a fixed -rate loan. 
Borrowers with outstanding loans are therefore protected. Obviously, new fixed loans are granted at higher rates. And this 
sharply contrasts with a floating-rates loans. 
Because with floating-rates loans, borrowers experience regular rate adjustments throughout the term of the loan. And in 
terms of bank financing? Strictly in terms of bank financing, an increase in short -term rates has an immediate and 
widespread restrictive impact on borrowers, while for long-term interest rates, the impact is more gradual, and it comes 
gradually as the outstanding amount of fixed-rate loans are progressively renewed. And the thing is that since the 
beginning of the war in Iran, we have seen a combination of the two.  
And in practical terms, the rate of new bank loans to non-financial corporations has increased about 25 basis points in the 
eurozone since the beginning of the year. 
0:16:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Just a few words about companies that borrow directly from the market.  
0:16:16 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, in that case, the impact is even more straightforward. The rate at which a company borrows from the market is the 
risk-free rate plus a premium. 
0:16:28 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Have risk premiums, known as corporate credit spreads, also increased?  
0:16:34 - Thomas Humblot 
Yes, they have increased temporarily at the beginning of the year but have returned to normal since. For the time being, the 
widening remains moderate for the highest-rated investment-grade issuers. 
However, some significant bond issuance in the euro debt market due to some major US technology companies has 
increased spreads for longer maturities. 
0:17:11 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In particular terms, how does this affect the investment decisions of the companies?  
0:17:19 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, investments that were previously worthwhile due to returns sufficient to cover the cost of capital may no longer do so 
and in turn that might lead to a decrease in demand for financing.  
0:17:40 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much Thomas. Now let us move on to the emerging economies. And it is with Cynthia Kalasopatan -Antoine.

### Page 6

6

Hello Cynthia, and welcome. 
0:17:54 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Hi, Emmanuel. Hello, everyone. 
0:17:57 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Long-term bond yields rose significantly in advanced economies over the summer. What can we now say about emerging 
markets? 
0:18:06 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Emerging bond markets held up well, which is quite unusual. The rise in long -term sovereign yields in emerging countries 
has been less pronounced than in advanced economies, including the United States and Germany. Now, there are two 
main exceptions, Turkey and Poland. Meanwhile, we did not witness any significant correction in emerging financial 
markets over the summer, and the main currencies appreciated slightly against the dollar or the euro.  
0:18:37 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Does this mean that public finances are more robust in emerging countries?  
0:18:43 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, it depends. You see, in cases like the Czech Republic and South Korea, public debt is moderate and macroeconomic 
fundamentals are solid. However, these countries are now less frequently considered as emerging and tend to be viewed 
as newly advanced economies. By contrast, Brazil and South Africa have weaker public finances and investors have 
already incorporated a higher sovereign risk into their assessments. And this is reflected in the bond yields. And this 
assessment of sovereign risk did not really change over the summer. 
0:19:26 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When comparing the main fiscal metrics, what are the trends? What do you see as an economist?  
0:19:33 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
On average, the fiscal metrics in emerging markets are not substantially worse than those in advanced economies. The 
average budget deficit levels are similar, ranging from 4% to 5% of GDP in 2025 -2026, according to the IMF. And as in 
advanced economies, the public debt is higher than its pre-Covid level. However, the average public debt to GDP ratio in 
emerging countries excluding China is close to 60%, whereas in advanced economies, it is above 100%.  
0:20:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Do you arrive at the same conclusion when comparing debt sustainability criteria?  
0:20:17 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, there are significant regional differences. In emerging Asia, the factors that determine debt sustainability are more 
often favorable than those in advanced economies. In Central Europe, the gap between the interest rate paid on debt and 
the GDP growth rate is also favorable. However, the primary deficits in some countries are high.  
0:20:45 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In a few words, a brief overview of the situation in Latin America.  
0:20:49 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, Brazil and Mexico are in a less favorable position. These countries would indeed need to generate a significant 
primary budget surplus each year in order to stabilize their debt in the medium term.

0:21:07 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Economists use the term original sin, which refers to the situation when governments resort to borrowing in foreign 
currency to address the shortfall in domestic financing. Does this phenomenon continue to distinguish emerging 
countries? 
0:21:24 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Yes, this is still the case. Emerging countries still have a significant share of their debt in foreign currency. This repres ents 
about 35 to 40% of their total debt on average. And in fact, now, this figure has decreased over the past decade, and this 
contributes to their resilience. However, governments in emerging markets have a less favorable position as regards their 
debt profile and their financing costs are also on average higher.  
0:22:02 - Emmanuel Laborde

0:22:07 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
China's public debt is significant and growing rapidly. That being said, it is denominated in domestic currency and issued in  
domestic markets. This debt is, however, sustainable in the medium term due to a very low effective interest rate and a 
high growth rate.

word is the good one, central banks intervene to curb this rise in interest rates?  
0:22:58 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Absolutely not. The main contribution that central banks can bring to dealing with this situation is to restore price stabili ty, 
as is their mandate, as promptly as possible. And in the current circumstances, this means keeping relatively high policy 
rates, in some cases, rising them further. And the responsibility for keeping public finances on a sustainable footing rests 
squarely with governments and any attempt by these governments to instead take the easy way out by pressuring central 
banks to lower interest rates prematurely would be enormously counterproductive.

Economy 
Inside the cost-of-living squeeze 
04 November 2026

Key takeaways 
• Not every essential has become less affordable. Despite the cumulative rise in prices, households are spending a slightly smaller 
share of income on gas and groceries than they did several years ago. That suggests the affordability squeeze is less broad-
based than the inflation headlines alone might imply. 
• Housing is the major exception, and the pressure extends beyond lower-income households. Both mortgages and rent have 
increased since 2019. Some lower-income renters look particularly stretched, but around one in four higher-income mortgage 
holders also spend more than half their income on housing. 
• Wage gains and changing behavior have helped households absorb higher prices. But real wage growth has mostly softened, 
which may help explain why sentiment remains weak even as consumer spending continues to grow. In fact, declining sentiment 
likely reflects the sacrifices consumers have to make in order to keep spending.

Everyday essentials take up less income than they did four years ago 
Higher prices are still top of mind for consumers, especially when it comes to affordability. But just how stretched are 
households? Using Bank of America internal data, we look at how much of their income households are spending on everyday 
essentials like gas, groceries, mortgages and rent, and how that has changed over the past four years across income groups. We 
also explore how wage growth and shifts in spending habits have helped households cope with higher prices, and why consumers 
still feel downbeat even as they keep spending. 
For example, while consumers overall spent slightly more of their income on gas and groceries compared to last year, outlays for 
these necessities are still below what they were four years ago, according to Bank of America payment and deposit data (Exhibit 
1). Combined, gas and groceries accounted for nearly 13.5% of income in 2026, 10 basis points more than last year, but almost 
half a percentage point less than four years ago. And even with price increases over the past year, most consumers only spent 
3.2% of their income on gasoline, up from 2.9% a year ago and below the 3.4% from four years ago.  
Exhibit 1: Consumers overall spent slightly more of their income on gas and groceries compared to last year, though spending on these necessities 
is still below pre-pandemic levels

Housing stress isn’t just a low-income story 
Housing is an altogether different picture. Whether you’re a homeowner or a renter, housing is often the biggest expense of any 
consumer, accounting for around 30% of income. Median mortgage payments have steadily risen over the past two years and 
now account for more than 30% of a homeowner’s income, according to Bank of America internal data (Exhibit 3). 
While renters have experienced some easing, they tend to set aside more of their income to housing costs (Exhibit 4). Rents as a 
share of income peaked in 2025, with renters spending more than 32% of their income on shelter. Even so, a small improvement 
helps. Due to declines in housing payments, renters have seen a significant improvement in discretionary spending over the past 
year (read more in On the move: Renovation over relocation?). Also, some housing related costs have also eased in the past year 
(read more in Energy bills: Short-term relief, longer-term challenges and Insurance costs hit the brakes).

rent, five percentage points more than higher earners (Exhibit 6).

Some financially stretched households may be particularly affected by rising housing costs 
Notably, in the first nine months of the year, roughly one in four lower-income renters spent more than half of their income on 
rent, up from about one in five in 2019 (Exhibit 7). One positive note, the share of renters who spend most of their income on 
rent does seem to have declined slightly in 2026.  
Financial pressure from housing is not limited to lower-income households. Around one in four higher-income mortgage holders 
spend more than half of their income on home payments (Exhibit 8). And while only slightly more than one in five lower-income 
mortgage holders spent half of their income on housing, this is a nearly five percentage point increase compared to pre-
pandemic levels.

Wage growth and changing habits are insulating some households from inflation 
Still, some families have not seen their bills, as a share of income, rise alongside inflation. Why is this? In our view, this is likely 
because after-tax wages have increased significantly for these people. This is especially true for some lower-income households 
that have landed steady employment since the pre-pandemic era, according to Bank of America internal data and the Consumer 
Price Index (CPI) (Exhibit 9).  
Additionally, consumers have adjusted their behavior in response to higher prices. For one, we may be driving less than before 
the pandemic. Despite some increases in vehicle miles driven per capita over the past four years, we’re still getting behind the 
wheel less on average compared to 2019 levels, according to the US Census Bureau and Bureau of Transportation (Exhibit 10). 
At the same time, some households have “traded down” to less expensive stores or items and services (read more in The rise of 
value).  
Exhibit 9: Some lower-income households have seen their wages 
grow faster than inflation since 2019 
After-tax wages, based on Bank of America deposit data (three-month 
moving average, index 2019 = 100) compared to CPI inflation, based on 
Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data (monthly, index 2019 = 100)

Source: Bank of America internal data, BLS 
Note: Data not available for October 2025 due to federal government shutdown. 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Exhibit 10: Vehicle miles driven per capita have not returned to pre-
pandemic levels 
Total vehicle miles traveled per total resident and civilian population 
estimates (monthly, index 2017 average = 100)

Source: US Census Bureau, Bureau of Transportation   
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Inflation is driving households to make more sacrifices 
There is one caveat to the affordability picture: wage growth among lower-income households slowed significantly throughout 
2025, though they’ve recovered since then. Furthermore, some middle- and higher-income families have not seen their pay keep 
up with inflation, at least compared to 2019 levels.  
In fact, using the ratio of after-tax wages to inflation as a proxy for “real wages,” we can see they largely declined throughout 
2022 and then again since mid-2023, according to Bank of America internal and BLS inflation data (Exhibit 11). Additionally, the 
drop in real wages is largely mirrored by a decline in the University of Michigan’s Index of Consumer Sentiment. This suggests 
that people have been feeling worse about their finances and the broader economy as their purchasing power has declined. 
Consumers are spending more than they did last year (read more in Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers), but sentiment 
likely reflects the sacrifices consumers have made in order to keep spending.  
Looking ahead, in our view, the outlook for affordability is relatively encouraging: wage growth remains firm and has picked up 
for lower-income houses. That said, there are risks. BofA Global Research notes the potential for gasoline and grocery prices to 
increase. And higher mortgage rates make it difficult for those looking to make the jump from renting to homeownership.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Grocery, as discussed in this analysis, includes spending at grocery stores, warehouse clubs and dollar/discount stores.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

Editorial 
Staying defensive and diversified  
Strategy summary:  Two weeks ago, we turned defensive 
towards equities, locking in semiconductor equity gains amid 
elevated bond volatility, narrowing market breadth and political 
uncertainty before the US mid -term elections. The near -term 
outlook reflected a less favourable risk-reward balance. Since 
we turned defensive , the Nasdaq 100 index has recorded its 
biggest weekly decline in three months as September’s hotter-
than-expected US inflation report briefly lifted bond yields. 
We have extended our defensive stance this week, 
downgrading US Communication Services equities to Neutral 
and upgrading US Healthcare to Overweight. We also closed 
our opportunistic bullish Communication Services idea and 
initiated a bullish idea on Healthcare, where earnings visibility 
and defensive characteristics appear more attractive. In fixed 
income, we see opportunities in US 10-year government bonds. 
We await clearer AI profit margins and spending guidance, 
broader earnings growth or lower bond and equity market 
volatility before turning positive on equities. Until then, 
diversification and disciplined entry levels remain important. 
Hot US inflation triggers equity pullback; not hot enough 
for an October Fed hike:  Risk assets pulled back after 
September’s hotter-than-expected inflation data (headline 3.7% 
y/y; core 2.6% y/y). Higher gasoline and diesel prices were the 
main drivers, but passthrough appeared in airline fares, used 
cars and transportation services, showing pressures were not 
confined to energy. Almost half of inflation components are still 
rising above 3%, a measure recently cited by Fed Chair Warsh. 
Producer inflation also beat estimates. The reports revive the 
possibility of an October Fed rate hike, but we expect a divided 
Fed to weigh it against September’s soft jobs report and 
narrowly hold this month. A December hike appears more likely 
if inflation remains firm . Although Fed Chair Warsh did not 
comment on policy at an IMF speech this week, New York Fed 
chief Williams said he saw no need to hike in 2027. Our base 
case remains two further hikes by June 2027. 
Limited equity pullback so far.  Equities fell sharply after the 
US inflation report, but the pullback is limited. The Nasdaq 100 
is 4.5% below its record high, while the S&P 500 remains close 
to all -time highs despite the latest decline. The S&P 500 
volatility index (VIX) has risen from below 15 to 1 8.2, still well 
under the 25 –30 range typically associated with capitulation 
and forced selling. Meanwhile, US government bond volatility 
(MOVE) remains elevated after surging since September.  Our 
stock-bond quant model turned more negativ e (-2) this week, 
signalling elevated equity volatility in the near term.  
When to turn more constructive on stocks?  We would turn 
more constructive when capitulation signals align: the Nasdaq 
100 approaches a 10% drawdown, the VIX rises to 25 –30 and 
our ‘Fear and Greed’ index  enters “extreme fear”. Greater 
confidence would also require easing concerns about AI 
spending and technology firms’ exposure to neocloud revenue, 
lower bond volatility and our stock-bond model turning positive 
again. Until these conditions emerge, volatility may remain 
elevated, favouring a defensive equity stance. Political risk 
should ease after the US mid -term elections. We continue to 
seek selective opportunities following excessive corrections. 
US financials remain a preferred sector after Q3 earnings:  
US large banks’ earnings were mixed, but underlying trends 
remain encouraging. Healthy loan and deposit growth supports 
net interest income, while resilient consumer spending, wealth 
management activity and a solid M&A and refinancing pipeline 
strengthen the outlook. Credit risks appear manageable . We 
remain constructive and favour stronger, diversified franchises. 
US government bonds offer value as yields spike:  The 
reversal in US government bond yields after their initial inflation-
driven spike suggests limited further upside. The 10 -year yield 
briefly reached 5.32% before slumping as falling equities 
prompted short-covering. We expect a near -term 5.00–5.25% 
range and view moves above 5.25% as attractive entry points 
for our opportunistic 10-year government bond idea. Structural 
pressures, including term premium and increasing supply to 
fund fiscal deficits and AI investments, prevent us from turning 
outright bullish on longer maturities. We, therefore, continue to 
prefer 3–7-year maturities in broader bond allocations.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya and Sundeep Gantori

### Page 4

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 16 October 2026

Top client questions 
  How does the September 2026 US Consumer Price Index 
(CPI) report alter your view on the USD and bond yields?   
Our view: The hotter-than-expected CPI print raises the odds of 
an October Fed hike, though we still expect a hold followed by 
a rate hike in December. In the near term, we maintain our view 
that the US 10 -year government bond yield will range between 
5.00% and 5.25%, while  the USD will remain supported as 
markets reassess the likelihood of further Fed hikes. Additional 
gains would depend partly on markets pricing a faster or more 
prolonged tightening cycle, while clearer signs of an 
approaching policy rate peak could see the USD ease back. 
Rationale: September US consumer inflation  came in above 
consensus across the board – headline CPI rose 3.7% y/y (vs. 3.6% 
estimate), and core CPI rose 2.6% y/y (vs. 2.5% estimate) – marking 
the fastest headline acceleration in five months. This shifts the 
balance of risks modestly hawkish . Unsurprisingly, the market -
implied probability of an October Fed hike rose over 50% overnight 
but closed the day at 32.7% – 9ppt higher than Tuesday’s close. 
We believe a split Fed will weigh this inflation report against the soft 
September jobs report and narrowly vote to keep rates on hold this 
month. Our base case remains for two more Fed hikes  by June 
2027. A December rate hike  now looks more like a done deal, 
especially as persistently higher oil prices keep headline and core 
inflation elevated for at least the next few months. Any re-escalation 
of the Middle East conflict after the  US mid-term elections would 
exert pressure on the Fed, although we believe Republicans would  
show greater restraint in US foreign action if they suffer steep Senate 
losses, which looks increasingly likely. 
The intra-day price action in US government bonds was instructive. 
The US 10-year yield briefly spiked to 5.32% on the print before 
closing materially lower, as pre -existing bearish positioning was 
unwound and Fed speakers (Barkin, Goolsbee) stopped well short 
of signalling an imminent move. This dynamic reinforces our view 
that the technical selling pressure driving yields towards the upper 
end of our range is fading. Structural drivers – term premium, fiscal 
supply concerns and ‘higher for longer’ Fed repricing – remain intact 
and keep us from turning outright bullish on duration. However, our 
opportunistic bullish idea in US 10-year government bonds initiated 
at September-end remains, as we continue to view spikes in US 10-
year yields over 5.25% as a good risk-reward entry. From a longer-
term portfolio standpoint, we continue to favour the 3-7-year tenor. 
On FX, elevated US government bond yields should sustain the 
USD’s interest rate appeal. However, expected hikes alone will not 
drive further appreciation. Stronger data or firmer Fed guidance 
would provide fresh support, while evidence that tightening is 
nearing completion would temper the USD’s strength. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Ray Heung, Senior Investment Strategist

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 16 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What are your key takeaways from US bank earnings? 
Our view: Q3 2026 earnings results from major US banks have 
been mixed, but underlying trends remain encouraging. Healthy 
loan and deposit growth, resilient consumer spending and 
wealth management activity continue to support the sector. 
Credit risks remain manageable. We remain constructive on the 
US financial sector, favouring stronger, diversified franchises. 
Rationale: Healthy loan and deposit growth continue to support net 
interest income (NII), hinting that the US economy remains resilient. 
Capital markets revenue grew year -on-year (y/y) , but moderated 
sequentially after a very strong Q2 2026, although performance 
varied across banks. Management commentary points to a healthy 
deal pipeline , supported by M&A activity and debt refinancing 
demand, although execution will depend on market conditions. 
Provisioning and net charge -off trends were mixed across banks, 
with no clear signs of broad deterioration in credit quality. Firmer 
US inflation raises the risk of further monetary tightening. While 
higher rates can support asset yields, they can also increase 
funding costs, pressure borrowers and weigh on securities ’ 
valuations. We will continue to monitor lending demand, credit 
costs, NII trends, capital markets momentum and capital returns.  
—  Robin Xie, Senior Investment Strategist 
  Can Big Tech’s earnings guidance sustain confidence in 
the AI investment cycle? 
Our view: We expect Big Tech’s AI spending to remain elevated, 
supporting our constructive AI capex outlook. However, we 
await guidance on the pace of capex growth and the 
sustainability of profit margins. 
Rationale: Semiconductor leaders’ latest company guidance points 
to supply constraints extending through 2028, supporting continued 
capacity expansion for compute . However, while AI capex is still 
likely to rise, y/y growth is expected to moderate  from c.100% in 
Q3 2026 to c.20% in Q3 2027. This normalisation in growth could 
raise concerns about the AI investment cycle’s durability.  
The Nasdaq-100 Index has rallied alongside accelerating AI capex. 
Slower capex growth is likely to moderate semiconductor revenue 
growth, while hyperscalers continue to incur depreciation costs on 
past investments. The slower growth is already partially reflected in 
the Nasdaq -100’s valuation de -rating this year. While we remain 
constructive on the AI investment theme, we prefer to stay 
prudent in the near term and await Big Tech’s guidance for greater 
visibility into spending plans in H2 2027 and beyond. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist

### Page 6

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 16 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  Would France face a credit rating downgrade by rating 
agencies this month? What might be the market implications? 
Our view: A rating downgrade is unlikely unless France’s 2027 
budget passes the National Assembly very soon with material 
changes to the proposal. We see French government bond 
yields remaining elevated relative to German Bund yields. 
Rationale: The French National Assembly began debating France’s 
2027 draft budget on 13 October 2026, with the first vote scheduled 
for 20 October. Near-term budget approval appears unlikely, as 
opposition parties have tabled motions to reject the proposal. The 
French Parliament has up to 70 days from the budget’s submission 
to complete the legislative process, although  its final adoption and 
the resolution of political challenges could extend into early 2027.  
Last October, two of the three major rating agencies downgraded 
France by one notch to the high-single-A category  with stable 
outlooks. The remaining agenc y rated France in the low-AA 
category with a negative outlook. As negative outlooks are typically 
resolved within 12 -18 months, agenc ies have time to await fiscal 
clarity before acting. We believe downgrade risks are largely priced 
in after the recent rise in yields, but volatility is likely to persist amid 
fiscal uncertainty and the approaching presidential election. Banks 
and domestically-focused issuers would face the greatest near-term 
pressure. However, we would view wider spreads as an opportunity 
to add exposure, particularly to French banks’ subordinated 
debt, given their stronger balance sheets and the fact that France 
contributes less than 50% of major French banks’ overall revenue. 
—  Ray Heung, Senior Investment Strategist 
  Are you still positive on Japanese banks? 
Our view: We are positive on Japanese banks despite the recent 
market correction, as the structural drivers of improving 
profitability and shareholder returns remain intact . While 
political risk and shifting BoJ rate hike odds could sustain near-
term volatility, we see the recent pullback as an opportunity to 
build selective exposure to Japanese bank stocks. 
Rationale: Japan’s gradual monetary policy normalisation  
should support banks’ NII as floating-rate loans reprice and maturing 
assets are reinvested at higher yields. Healthy corporate lending 
demand, improving capital efficiency and continued share buybacks 
provide further support . While uncertainty over the pace of BoJ 
tightening could weigh on sentiment, we see gradual interest rate 
normalisation as supportive of medium-term bank profitability.  
Key risks  include rising deposit costs, mark -to-market losses on 
bond portfolios and a disorderly increase in Japanese government 
bond yields. We continue to favour well-capitalised banks  with 
strong domestic deposit franchises and improving returns on equity. 
—  Robin Xie, Senior Investment Strategist

### Page 7

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 16 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  Could NZD/USD rebound from its one-year trough? If so, 
how far could it recover?  
Our view: New Zealand’s improving domestic conditions, 
persistent inflation and technical support provide a basis for 
NZD/USD’s initial recovery towards the 0.57 level, although 
elevated US bond yields could slow its progress. 
Rationale: In Q3, New Zealand’s  seasonally-adjusted business 
confidence reading rose to a net 40% of firms expecting the economy 
to improve, up from net 14% in Q2 . Hiring intentions improved, and 
filled jobs rose 0.2% m/m in August. These developments suggest 
domestic conditions remain supportive for the NZD.  
New Zealand’s above-target inflation keeps further RBNZ tightening 
in focus. The 22 October consumer inflation data release and the 
28 October RBNZ policy meeting are key catalysts: persistent price 
pressures a nd improving demand are expected to prompt firmer 
policy guidance and strengthen the NZD’s interest-rate appeal. 
NZD/USD’s position near the lower boundary of its downtrend 
channel suggests scope for a technical rebound. We see 0.57 as 
the first recovery hurdle; a sustained break  above this level  would 
strengthen the case for further gains. Firmer US  consumer inflation 
remains a headwind, making a gradual recovery more plausible 
than a sharp reversal.  
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  Can the proposed Russia -Ukraine ‘energy ceasefire ’ 
contain oil prices as Middle East risks rise? 
Our view: We expect escalating Middle East tensions to keep oil 
prices elevated . We  maintain our  three- and 12 -month West 
Texas Intermediate (WTI) oil price forecasts at USD 100/bbl and 
USD 80/bbl, respectively, and continue to favour US Treasury 
Inflation-Protected Securities (TIPS) as a portfolio hedge 
against persistent inflation risks. 
Rationale: The proposed Russia-Ukraine ‘energy ceasefire’ still 
looks some way off. In our view, even if an agreement is reached, it 
would do little to ease the supply risks building in the Middle East. 
Gulf oil exports have recovered to above 80% of pre-conflict levels 
in recent weeks, helping moderate the pace of price increases. 
However, the recovery remains vulnerable to disruptions, particularly 
following the latest Houthi attacks on Saudi energy infrastructure . 
Diesel and jet fuel remain under the most pressure , with Gulf 
exports of these fuels in September  at only around 60% of pre -
conflict levels. A sustained disruption to major Saudi production or 
export infrastructure remains the principal upside risk to oil prices. 
—  Anthony Naab, Investment Strategist

Document 445: United States _ A shock-resistant expansion (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/United States _ A shock-resistant expansion (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

6
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
A SHOCK-RESISTANT EXPANSION
US growth remains resilient despite successive shocks. According to our forecasts, it is expected to reach 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027. Its main driver is still investment in artificial intelligence (AI), which has remained entirely unaffected by the
energy crisis. The labour market has seen a marked improvement in job creation and a fall in the unemployment rate. Core

inflation remains stubbornly high and above the Federal Reserve (Fed)’s target. Against this backdrop, the Fed has begun a
hiking cycle in September, which, according to our scenario, would bring the Fed Funds target to 4.50% (upper limit) by Q1
2027. At the same time, long-term rates are under upward pressure.
1  This includes: energy and communications; data centres (facilities); IT equipment and peripherals; communication equipment (equipment); software; and research and develop-

ment (intellectual-property products).
THE ENERGY SHOCK IS NOT HOLDING BACK GROWTH
Driven by AI-related investment, US growth is holding up despite ri -
sing energy inflation. We forecast GDP growth at 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027 on an annual average basis. The AI-related investment
cycle1 (+14.8% y/y in H1 2026) is expected to continue, accompanied by
potential productivity gains. However, other private-investment seg -
ments (including residential property) remain sluggish. In addition, the

rising cost of capital could accentuate its ‘K-shaped’ profile. Household
consumption is expected, on the whole, to benefit from the rebound in
job creation and to remain above 2% y/y through to the end of 2027.
In H1 2026, it held up against resurging inflation due to an average
year-on-year increase of 11.5% in tax refunds and a savings rate stan-
ding at its lowest level since 2022 (2.8% in Q2).
RECORD CAPITAL-RAISING DRIVEN BY AI
This year, AI-related investments are driving massive capital-raising

by companies through loans, bond issues or share placements. This
trend is set to continue given the seemingly colossal scale of the fun -
ding requirements, even as risk premia in the bond market begin to
widen. According to their statements, the five tech giants could invest
nearly USD 900 bn this year and over USD 1,000 bn next year—more
than double the figure for 2025. Spurred on by regulatory easing, the
major US banks have announced that they will mobilise significant re-

sources to support projects considered strategic for the United States
(AI, defence, energy and infrastructure). While funding for the AI boom
appears unaffected by rising long-term interest rates, these latter are
likely to continue to weigh on household demand for mortgages. Since
the start of the conflict in the Middle East, 30-year mortgage rates
have risen by 78 bp (to 6.76% at the start of September). Conversely,
consumer lending remained buoyant in H1 2026 and is supporting

household spending.
TWO-SPEED GROWTH IN INDUSTRY
Industrial production rebounded slightly in 2025 (+1.1%), driven by AI
and aerospace. We forecast an acceleration to +1.5% in 2026 and +2%
in 2027. Investment in AI is bolstering the electronics sector, while the
(civil and military) aerospace sector is rebounding as order backlogs
are cleared and defence requirements rise. Conversely, sectors ex -
posed to rising tariffs (in particular, the steel, aluminium and auto -

motive sectors) are still on the back burner, contrary to the objective
of these measures. This positive momentum in industrial production,
while concentrated, is expected to continue, bolstered by the recove -
ry in business sentiment. The ISM Manufacturing Index has been in
expansionary territory since early 2026, despite ongoing pressures on
prices and supply chains.

### Page 2

7
EcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
LABOUR MARKET: A LOW UNEMPLOYMENT RATE ON A DOWNWARD
TREND
Job creation (non-farm payrolls) has rebounded, averaging +80,000 per
month since the start of the year, compared with +10,000 in 2025. This
growth is no longer limited to the healthcare sector. Excluding health-
care, job creation is up by an average of +38,000 in 2026, compared

with -47,000 in 2025. Manufacturing and construction, two sectors
driven by AI, are contributing to this rebound. At the same time, the
labour force has not grown since January 2025 due to the decline in the
foreign-born workforce (down 4.2% cumulatively over the period). Ex -
cluding the impact of COVID-19, the labour force participation rate is at
its lowest level since the 1970s, standing at around 61.5%. The rebound
in labour demand and persistent pressure on the labour supply have

brought the unemployment rate down to 4.1% in August 2026 (a 0.3 pp
fall since the start of the year). The jobless rate is expected to fall to
3.9% at the end of 2027 (4.2% corresponds to full employment, accor -
ding to the CBO) thanks to a job creation rate of around 40,000–60,000
per month.
INFLATION: STRUCTURALLY ABOVE TARGET
The rise in oil prices has triggered a rebound in CPI inflation. The
contribution from the energy component (+1.2 pp) largely accounts for

the rise in inflation since February. A slowdown was seen (from +4.2%
to +3.4% y/y) between the May peak and August. This is expected to
continue due to base effects on energy prices. We anticipate the trough
to be +1.8% y/y in Q2 2027 before a stabilisation at around +2.3% y/y
later in the year.
Meanwhile, core inflation is expected to remain above the Fed’s 2%
target, and above +2.5% y/y in 2027. This highlights the structural
nature of inflation, beyond short-term fluctuations linked to energy.

Non-housing services are expected to show significant inertia, with in-
flation hovering at around +3% y/y by the end of 2027. The potential for
disinflation also appears limited for housing-related services, as the
rise in rents shows no signs of abating. Finally, inflation on non-energy
goods is slowing as the effects of tariffs fade. However, the AI cycle is
exerting upward pressure through the general rise in the cost of elec -
tronic goods.
FED: GREEN LIGHT FOR RATE HIKES

The FOMC has embarked on a cycle of rate increases. The Fed Funds
target was raised to 4.0% (+25 bp) at the September meeting, and we
anticipate two further rises that would bring the policy rate to 4.5% in
January 2027. These three rises (made possible by reassuring develop-
ments in employment and economic activity) are intended to reverse
the three cuts in 2025 adopted to counter the ‘downside risks’ to the
labour market at that time. As a result, the committee can recalibrate

its policy towards ‘price stability’ and, through its interest rate deci -
sions, reflect the priority given to this objective in its communications.
The risks point towards further monetary tightening: in our baseline
scenario, core inflation would remain sticky and the fall in unemploy -
ment would give the FOMC room for manoeuvre to raise rates further.
PUBLIC FINANCES: UNDERLYING DETERIORATION AND PRESSURE ON
LONG- TERM RATES
According to our estimates, the federal deficit is set to widen by 0.7 pp

to -6.6% of GDP in 2026 (by 0.4 pp to -3.1% for the primary deficit),
weighed down by falling tariff revenue (refunds of tariffs already col -
lected and lower-than-expected receipts). In 2027, the end of this one-
off effect is expected to lead to a marginal improvement to -6.3% (to
-2.8% for the primary deficit). The growing burden of interest payments
(3.5% of GDP in 2026 and 3.6% in 2027, following 3.2% in 2025) and
the conflict in Iran (affecting the military budget) are weighing on the

budget balance and federal-debt dynamics. Federal debt is still on an
upward trend, with no particular change in direction, exceeding 100% of
GDP (101.9%, up 3.3 pp, in 2026, and 103.5% in 2027). It is expected to
reach 110% by the end of the decade against a backdrop of budget de-
ficits of close to 6% of GDP , assuming fiscal policy remains unchanged.
Furthermore, the mid-term elections (which see the entire House of
Representatives and one-third of the Senate up for re-election) could

result in a divided Congress or in both chambers changing hands to
the Democratic Party. Should the former occur, this would increase the
risk of a legislative deadlock, while the presidential veto would limit
the Democrats’ ability to influence fiscal policy in the latter scenario.
Against this backdrop, pressure on the bond market is likely to continue
in the coming months. Treasury issuance is rising, fuelled by increa -
sing spending requirements (in areas such as AI, defence and supply

chains). At the same time, shifts in investor demographics are increa-
sing demand volatility, and issuances by companies in the artificial
intelligence sector are intensifying competition for capital. We antici -
pate a 10-year yield of 4.95% in Q1 2027, before a slight easing brought
about by Fed rate rises, which would lend credibility to the price-sta -
bility objective. However, according to our forecast, 10-year yield is
expected to remain close to a 20-year high, at 4.80% in Q4 2027.

THE US RELIES ON IMPORTS FOR ITS GROWTH
The US trade deficit has recently started to rise again. According to
our forecasts, it is expected to account for the same proportion of GDP
in 2026 as it did in 2024 (4.4%). This trend is driven by imports of
AI-related capital goods, which are set to rise from 1.2% to 2.1% of GDP
between 2022 and 2026. Investment in AI is, in fact, import-intensive
(Chart 2), which dampens its positive impact on growth and highlights

the US’s dependence on foreign supplies in a cycle that it is neverthe-
less spearheading.
The additional tariffs have failed to reverse the trend in the trade de -
ficit. Furthermore, the IEEPA tariffs struck down by the Supreme Court
were replaced, in July 2026, by ‘Section 301’ tariffs (forced labour) ran-
ging from 10% to 12.5%. These tariffs target 60 countries and have
been introduced on top of existing sector-specific tariffs (steel, alumi -

nium and automotive). Following these adjustments, the statutory rate
(measured against the 2024 import structure) remained slightly above
10%. The main risks relate to potential new sector-specific tariffs and
the renegotiation of the USMCA.
Article completed on 17 September 2026
Anis Bensaidani
anis.bensaidani@bnpparibas.com
Céline Choulet
celine.choulet@bnpparibas.com

Document 463: Westpac Wave - Fiji Quarterly Economic Update (2026-11-06)

FIJI ECONOMIC UPDATE AND OUTLOOK

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
FIJI ECONOMIC UPDATE
Fiji’s resilience is being tested...
Fiji’s economy entered 2026 with growth already normalising
from the post-pandemic recovery. Real GDP growth slowed from
3.6% in 2024 to 2.5% in 2025. Foreign reserves started the year

around $3.7bn, banking-system liquidity remained high and the
RBF maintained its Overnight Policy Rate at 0.25%.
Activity so far into 2026 has been somewhat stronger than what
we feared. Visitor arrivals reached 762,352 during January–
September, up 3.7%yr, while construction, investment lending,
cement sales, remittances and private-sector credit remained
firm. Nevertheless, Westpac Fiji has kept its 2026 growth
forecast unchanged at 2.0%. Stronger headline indicators are

not translating one-for-one into real economic growth because
higher prices and intermediate costs are absorbing a growing
share of nominal activity.
The outlook changed materially in March as the Middle East
conflict disrupted global energy markets. Fiji remains heavily
dependent on imported petroleum products, so higher refined-
fuel, freight and insurance costs flowed through transport,
electricity, food distribution and wider business expenses. By

June, motor spirit price reached $3.93/litre and diesel at a record
$4.58/litre, while automotive-fuel sales value rose 24.4%yr in the
June quarter.
As this report goes out, the Middle East conflict remains a
key external downside risk. At least 12 vessels were attacked
in the week ending 16 October and yet it is reported that oil
flows through Hormuz averaged 11.6mn barrels per day to 13
October, about 68% of pre-war levels through alternative and

costly routes. Iran says a seven-day reopening proposal remains
available, but US-Iran negotiations remain unresolved.
Westpac Economics’ most recent scenario analysis “ Persistent
energy pressures have further to run ” indicates that relief will
be gradual. Brent crude is forecast to average US$101/bbl in Q4
2026 before easing through 2027. Refined products are expected
to normalise more slowly because refinery capacity, inventories,
freight and insurance remain constrained. Under Westpac’s

escalation scenario, in a return to open conflict Brent could reach
around US$150/bbl in H1 2027.
Domestic tourism sector demonstrates the nominal-versus-
real growth tension. June-quarter visitor arrivals increased to
287,588, yet earnings fell 4.9%yr to $708.1mn. First-half tourism
earnings declined 1.7%yr despite higher arrivals, even though
hotel takings increased 9.5%yr.
Australia and New Zealand remain critical, together contributing
around 71% of September arrivals. Westpac expects Australian

growth to slow from 2.1% in 2026 to 1.5% in 2027, while New
Zealand strengthens from 1.9% to 2.6%. Tourism therefore
increasingly needs to focus on yield rather than visitor numbers
alone.
Shamal Chand
Senior Economist, Westpac Pacific Economics

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
FIJI ECONOMIC UPDATE
...but the buffers are still doing the heavy lifting...
There are good reasons why growth held up despite these shocks.
Construction work put in place reached $155.9mn in the June
quarter, up 8.6%yr, while investment lending, cement sales and

construction-related imports subsequently remained strong.
Industrial production increased 1.5%yr, although performance
remained uneven across industries.
Inward personal remittances have been particularly impressive,
reaching $1.2bn during January–August, up 32.7%yr. We expect
remittances to reach $1.55bn this year. These inflows, with strong
diaspora support, are helping households absorb higher living
costs while simultaneously supporting Fiji’s external position.

However, the external accounts show the cost of this resilience.
Fiji’s merchandise trade deficit widened to $2.9bn in the first half,
with imports reaching $4.3bn against exports of $1.4bn. Mineral-
fuel imports alone reached around $1.2bn, up 57%yr. Foreign
reserves nevertheless recovered to approximately $4.09bn by
end-September, equivalent to 5.7 months of retained imports , also
supported by Government’s external loan draw-down.
Our in-house import-demand indicator is signalling softer

domestic momentum. Import activity over the three months
to September was down 21.3%yr and fell 39.8% compared
with the preceding three months. The broader six-month
measure declined at a more moderate 16.1% annualised pace.
While volatile, the trend adds to our view that higher prices are
weighing on real activity, despite continued strength across
tourism, remittances, credit and construction.
Headline inflation reached 7.6%yr in August before easing to

6.8% in September, with transport prices up 12.2%yr and food
prices up 7.6%. Our latest forecast has November inflation
around 8.0%yr, average inflation around 4.3% for 2026 and
December (year-end) inflation at 7.4%.
Higher fuel prices are no longer affecting only households.
The shock is moving through freight, electricity, agriculture,
construction materials and wider supply chains. For instance,
we note recent increases in taxi and bus fares. These higher

intermediate costs compress business margins and reduce
household purchasing power.
This also informs our 2027 view. Westpac Fiji has downgraded
its 2027 growth forecast to 2.5%, reflecting the prevailing
effects of the energy crisis, elevated refined-fuel prices and the
broader transmission of higher intermediate costs into next year.
Westpac’s global outlook supports this caution: crude prices are
expected to ease gradually, but refined products could remain

comparatively expensive for longer.
Renewable energy is increasingly important because the fuel
crisis has put a clear economic price on Fiji’s imported-energy
dependence. The faster Fiji prioritises a shift to renewable energy,
the sooner it can establish a more stable energy foundation.
El Niño creates an additional risk through agriculture, hydro
generation and food prices. The buffers remain meaningful, but
they are being asked to work significantly harder.

### Page 4

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
4
WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
FIJI ECONOMIC UPDATE
...as risks converge
Government finances add another dimension to the outlook.
The FY2025/26 deficit came in at $597.9mn, or 4.3% of GDP,
substantially better than originally budgeted.
Nevertheless, financing requirements are substantial.

Government plans to raise $1.48bn in FY2026/27, comprising
$1.046bn in deficit financing and $436.3mn refinancing. Around
$853.9mn to be domestically sourced and $627.95mn externally,
including a $444mn ADB policy-based loan in November.
Domestic yields therefore deserve closer attention. Fiji’s
government yield curve has steepened considerably, with the
adjustment concentrated beyond 10 years. While the 10-year
yield remained at 3.90%, the 20-year yield rose by 80 basis points

between July 2025 and September 2026 to 5.80%. The 25-year
yield increased even more sharply, climbing from 5.94% to 7.19%
after peaking at 7.25% in mid-2026.
Pressure is also emerging at the short end. The Government’s
20 October Treasury Bill tender offered $50mn across three-,
six- and 12-month maturities. Investors bid for the full amount,
but sought yields of 0.35%, 0.60% and 1.30%, respectively,
above the listed yields of 0.30%, 0.55% and 1.25%. Government

did not accept the higher yields, leaving the entire $50mn offer
unallocated. This suggests investors are increasingly seeking
better returns even on shorter-dated Government securities.
The financial system still provides considerable protection.
Banking-system liquidity stood around $2.28bn at end-
September and the RBF maintained its OPR at 0.25%. In August,
the weighted-average lending rate was 4.51%, the savings-
deposit rate 0.31% and the time-deposit rate 2.01%.

For now, high liquidity limits immediate crowding-out risks.
Higher government yields may encourage banks and institutional
investors to allocate more funds towards government securities.
If liquidity subsequently tightens, deposit rates may need to rise
to retain savings, adding pressure to bank funding costs and
eventually lending rates.
S&P’s decision to maintain Fiji’s B+/B ratings but revise the
outlook from stable to negative reinforces the importance of

debt sustainability, investor confidence and credible medium-
term fiscal consolidation.
Political uncertainty adds another layer, with ongoing public
debate on constitutional amendments and the general election
due by 6 February 2027.
This brings us back to the growth story. We have maintained
2026 growth at 2.0% because resilient tourism, remittances,
construction and investment are being offset by unexpectedly
high inflation and intermediate costs. Our downgrade of 2027

growth to 2.5% recognises that this adjustment will take longer
than previously expected, while fiscal, financial, energy, climate
and political risks increasingly overlap.

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
INFLATION OUTLOOK
Cost pressures move beyond the pump
Inflation has emerged as a key pressure point for Fiji, threatening 
to erode gains from otherwise resilient domestic activity. 
Headline inflation accelerated to 6.8%yr in September, driven by 
essentials: food prices rose 7.6%yr, transport increased 12.2%yr 
and housing and utilities were 4.9% higher. The underlying 
picture remains less severe, with prices declining 0.4%mth in 
September and annual-average inflation at 1.9%. Nevertheless, 
the acceleration suggests the global fuel shock is increasingly 
working its way through the domestic economy.

The import-price pipeline reinforces this view. Import prices 
increased 4.9%qtr and 4.4%yr in the June quarter, including 
a 15.4%yr rise in mineral-product prices as petrol, diesel and 
aviation-fuel costs increased. Export prices rose 6.2%yr, although 
the terms of trade eased 0.6%qtr. Elevated energy costs are 
also spreading beyond the pump. Transport-services producer 
prices increased 2.7%qtr in June, while building-material prices 
rose 1.5%qtr in September, signalling broader pressure across 
distribution, construction and services.

Fuel-price pressures intensified again in October, when regulated 
unleaded prices in Viti Levu increased to $3.15/litre and diesel 
rose by 21 cents to $3.41/litre. Prices nevertheless remain below 
their June peaks. Our indicative estimates point to further 
increases in fuel prices over the coming months.

The global outlook offers little immediate relief. Westpac 
Economics expects Brent to average US$101/bbl in Q4 2026 
before easing to US$77/bbl by Q4 2027, but refined fuels are 
expected to ease more slowly as refinery supply remains more 
constrained, while shipping, insurance and inventory pressures 
gradually normalise. Westpac’s Australian forecasts provide 
some regional context, with diesel and petrol expected to 
average 274c/l and 230c/l, respectively, in Q4 before declining 
through 2027.

For Fiji, the distinction between crude and refined-product prices 
is important. Singapore gasoil prices, freight and insurance costs 
are expected to remain elevated and ease only gradually through 
2027, meaning lower Brent prices may not translate immediately 
into cheaper imported fuel. Higher diesel costs feed into freight, 
construction, agriculture and distribution, while aviation-
fuel pressures add to airline operating costs and tourism-
sector margins. Repeated shocks increase the likelihood that 
businesses eventually pass more of these costs on to consumers, 
particularly through food and other essentials.

We consequently expect inflation to remain elevated through 
year-end, rising towards 8.0% in November before easing to 7.4% 
in December, taking annual-average inflation to around 4.3%. 
The concern is increasingly that an imported fuel shock develops 
into broader cost-push inflation into next year. The squeeze on 
real purchasing power makes inflation an increasingly important 
downside risk to Fiji’s growth outlook heading into 2027.
Source: FBoS, Macrobond, Westpac Economics

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
CREDIT RATING OUTLOOK
S&P negative outlook is concerning
Fiji’s sovereign credit profile has come under renewed pressure
recently, adding another layer of caution to an economy already
navigating softer real growth and elevated external risks.

S&P maintained Fiji’s long-term rating at B+ and short-term
rating at B but revised the outlook from Stable to Negative.
While not a downgrade, it signals increased risk if fiscal
and debt metrics deteriorate. Historically, four of Fiji’s six
negative outlooks since 2006 have resulted in downgrades,
typically during periods of major economic or fiscal stress.
Similar vulnerabilities are resurfacing, with rising government
expenditure and guarantees, alongside Middle East tensions and

inflationary pressures.
The key concern is fiscal. S&P viewed the 2026/27 Budget as
more expansionary than previously expected, effectively slowing
the fiscal consolidation achieved in previous years. It projects
the fiscal deficit to average 4.6% of GDP over 2027–29, including
4.9% in FY2027, with net government debt moving above 70% of
GDP. Once contingent liabilities are incorporated, the broader
public-sector exposure approaches 80% of GDP. This leaves

considerably less fiscal space to absorb another major domestic
or external shock.
In S&P’s latest assessment, contingent liabilities are becoming
increasingly important to the sovereign risk story. Government
exposure through guarantees and other contingent liabilities
adds to the broader fiscal risk, while a widening current account
deficit, elevated imports and rising external debt reinforce the
risks surrounding the outlook. For Fiji, this matters because

the next phase of growth will require substantial investment in
infrastructure, climate resilience and productive capacity.
The negative outlook therefore strengthens the case for a more
credible medium-term fiscal consolidation path by the Fiji
Government. S&P indicated that the outlook could return to
Stable if economic growth and tourism outperform its forecasts,
resulting in narrower fiscal deficits and lower debt ratios, or if
improvements in institutional and policy settings sustainably

strengthen fiscal outcomes and debt levels.
Importantly, the assessment across rating agencies is not
uniformly negative. Moody’s maintained Fiji’s B1 long-term issuer
rating with a Stable outlook, suggesting that despite significant
structural and fiscal challenges, the overall credit profile remains
broadly unchanged and risks remain balanced.
A future downgrade would likely reinforce perceptions of
higher sovereign risk just as Fiji requires greater private and

foreign investment. Maintaining fiscal credibility, containing
contingent liabilities and ensuring that new borrowing supports
productivity-enhancing investment will be increasingly
important. With elevated uncertainty, climate vulnerability and
external shocks remaining additional downside risks, the room
for further fiscal slippage is becoming increasingly limited for the
Fijian Government.

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
SUPER EL NIÑO
The threat of a “very strong” El Niño
It is the year of the ‘very strong’ El Niño, and for Fiji the risk is no
longer just on the horizon. Dry conditions are already affecting
parts of the country, while the Government has stepped up

preparations for a potentially more difficult period ahead.
History suggests this matters for the broader economy because
prolonged drought can simultaneously affect agriculture, water
supply and electricity generation before flowing through food
prices, imports, rural incomes and ultimately growth.
Our rainfall analysis covering 1981–2025 shows that rainfall
during El Niño periods was 24.7% lower than under neutral
conditions nationally, with all four regions recording historical

deficits above 20%.
During the 1982–83 El Niño, severe drought coincided with
Cyclone Oscar and a weak global economy. Historical estimates
highlight that Fiji’s economy contracted by 4.0%, against a
pre-disaster forecast of 2.7% growth during the super El Niño
period then. The 1997–98 episode was more severe. By 1998 the
cane crop was forecast at just 1.88mn tonnes, producing around
150,000 tonnes of sugar. Historical estimates used here indicate

cane production fell around 26%, contributing to an aggregate
GDP contraction of at least 1.3%. Fiji also devalued the dollar by
20% in January 1998 amid the overlapping Asian Financial Crisis.
The social cost was significant. In 1998, around 27,562
households needed food relief and 47,592 were already receiving
emergency water supplies according to some surveys. The 2015–
16 episode again brought crop losses and drying water supplies.
The effects are beginning to show

The current event is already leaving a footprint. September
rainfall was drier than usual across the Western, Central and
Eastern divisions and most of the Northern Division, while several
Western Division stations recorded less than 100mm.
More recent monitoring points to serious or very dry conditions
across parts of Vanua Levu, Taveuni, Lomaiviti, Yasawa, Lau and
other areas. Local effects are becoming tangible. Taveuni had
already required assistance with water carting in September,

while NDRMO reported that Fiji was experiencing meteorological
drought and warned it could progress towards hydrological and
agricultural drought if conditions continued.
The Government’s response has consequently shifted towards
preparedness. The National Disaster Risk Management Council
is reviewing the National El Niño Preparedness and Response
Plan. Humanitarian clusters were also instructed to prepare
contingency plans for consolidation and presentation to Cabinet,

while NDRMO has been working with Energy Fiji Limited and the
Water Authority of Fiji to assess emerging risks.
This marks a progression from earlier in the year. In August,
our assessment was that the FY2026–27 Budget contained
no explicit El Niño preparedness measure, implying much
of the initial response would need to occur through existing
institutional arrangements.
The economic transmission remains important. Agriculture feels
reduced rainfall first through weaker soil moisture and water

availability. Lower domestic food production can then lift prices
and import requirements. Electricity provides another channel
as weaker hydro generation could require increased thermal
generation and imported fuel, adding to higher production
costs and foreign-exchange outflows. And Fiji has less room for
another supply-side shock.
Fiji is more diversified than during the droughts of the 1980s
and 1990s, while its reserve position provides an important

buffer. But financial buffers cannot replace rainfall, agricultural
production or water supplies.
